<p><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg/50px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="42" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg/75px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg/100px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1516" data-file-height="1265" /></span></span>

<span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/List_of_coats_of_arms_of_Prime_Ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="List of coats of arms of Prime Ministers of the United Kingdom"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Arms_of_Tony_Blair.svg/30px-Arms_of_Tony_Blair.svg.png" decoding="async" width="30" height="36" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Arms_of_Tony_Blair.svg/45px-Arms_of_Tony_Blair.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Arms_of_Tony_Blair.svg/60px-Arms_of_Tony_Blair.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="463" data-file-height="557" /></a></span></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Tony_Blair_sidebar" title="Template:Tony Blair sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Tony_Blair_sidebar" title="Template talk:Tony Blair sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Tony_Blair_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Sir Anthony Charles Lynton Blair</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_the_Garter" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of the Garter">KG</a></span> (born 6 May 1953) is a British politician who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> from 1997 to 2007 and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Leader of the Labour Party (UK)">Leader of the Labour Party</a> from 1994 to 2007. He served as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(United_Kingdom)" title="Leader of the Opposition (United Kingdom)">Leader of the Opposition</a> from 1994 to 1997 and had various <a href="/wiki/Shadow_cabinet" title="Shadow cabinet">shadow cabinet</a> posts from 1987 to 1994. Blair was <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(United_Kingdom)" title="Member of Parliament (United Kingdom)">Member of Parliament</a> (MP) for <a href="/wiki/Sedgefield_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Sedgefield (UK Parliament constituency)">Sedgefield</a> from 1983 to 2007. He was the second longest-serving prime minister in post-war British history after <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a> and the longest-serving <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour</a> politician to have held the office.
</p><p>Blair attended the independent school <a href="/wiki/Fettes_College" title="Fettes College">Fettes College</a>, studied law at <a href="/wiki/St_John%27s_College,_Oxford" title="St John&#39;s College, Oxford">St John's College, Oxford</a>, and qualified as a barrister. He became involved in the Labour Party and was elected to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> in 1983 for the Sedgefield constituency in <a href="/wiki/County_Durham" title="County Durham">County Durham</a>. As a <a href="/wiki/Backbencher" title="Backbencher">backbencher</a>, Blair supported moving the party to the <a href="/wiki/Political_centre_of_British_politics" class="mw-redirect" title="Political centre of British politics">political centre of British politics</a>. He was appointed to <a href="/wiki/Neil_Kinnock" title="Neil Kinnock">Neil Kinnock</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Cabinet_of_Neil_Kinnock" title="Shadow Cabinet of Neil Kinnock">shadow cabinet</a> in 1988 and was appointed <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Home_Secretary" title="Shadow Home Secretary">Shadow Home Secretary</a> by <a href="/wiki/John_Smith_(Labour_Party_leader)" title="John Smith (Labour Party leader)">John Smith</a> in 1992. Following Smith's death, Blair won <a href="/wiki/1994_Labour_Party_leadership_election" title="1994 Labour Party leadership election">a leadership election in 1994</a> to succeed him. As leader, Blair began a historic rebranding of the party, which became known as "<a href="/wiki/New_Labour" title="New Labour">New Labour</a>".
</p><p>Blair became the youngest prime minister of the 20th century after winning the <a href="/wiki/1997_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1997 United Kingdom general election">1997 general election</a>, Labour's largest general election victory in history. During <a href="/wiki/First_Blair_ministry" title="First Blair ministry">his first term</a>, Blair enacted constitutional reforms and significantly increased <a href="/wiki/Public_finance" title="Public finance">public spending</a> on healthcare and education while also introducing controversial market-based reforms in these areas. In addition, Blair saw the introduction of a <a href="/wiki/Minimum_wage" title="Minimum wage">minimum wage</a>, tuition fees for higher education, <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_amendment" title="Constitutional amendment">constitutional reform</a> such as <a href="/wiki/Devolution_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Devolution in the United Kingdom">devolution in Scotland and Wales</a>, an extensive expansion of <a href="/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="LGBT rights in the United Kingdom">LGBT rights in the UK</a>, and significant progress in the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_peace_process" title="Northern Ireland peace process">Northern Ireland peace process</a> with the passing of the landmark <a href="/wiki/Good_Friday_Agreement" title="Good Friday Agreement">Good Friday Agreement</a>. On foreign policy, Blair oversaw British interventions in <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_War" title="Kosovo War">Kosovo in 1999</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone_Civil_War" title="Sierra Leone Civil War">Sierra Leone in 2000</a>, which were generally perceived to be successful. Blair was <a href="/wiki/2001_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="2001 United Kingdom general election">re-elected in a second landslide in 2001</a>. Three months into <a href="/wiki/Second_Blair_ministry" title="Second Blair ministry">his second term</a>, Blair's premiership was shaped by the <a href="/wiki/9/11_terrorist_attacks" class="mw-redirect" title="9/11 terrorist attacks">9/11 terrorist attacks</a>, resulting in the start of the <a href="/wiki/War_on_terror" title="War on terror">war on terror</a>. Blair supported the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_George_W._Bush_administration" title="Foreign policy of the George W. Bush administration">foreign policy of the George W. Bush administration</a> by ensuring that the <a href="/wiki/British_Armed_Forces" title="British Armed Forces">British Armed Forces</a> participated in the <a href="/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)" title="War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)">War in Afghanistan</a> to overthrow the <a href="/wiki/Taliban" title="Taliban">Taliban</a>, destroy <a href="/wiki/Al-Qaeda" title="Al-Qaeda">al-Qaeda</a>, and capture <a href="/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden" title="Osama bin Laden">Osama bin Laden</a>. In 2003, Blair supported the <a href="/wiki/2003_invasion_of_Iraq" title="2003 invasion of Iraq">invasion of Iraq</a> and had the British Armed Forces participate in the <a href="/wiki/Iraq_War" title="Iraq War">Iraq War</a>, on the erroneous claims that <a href="/wiki/Saddam_Hussein" title="Saddam Hussein">Saddam Hussein</a>'s regime <a href="/wiki/Iraq_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction" title="Iraq and weapons of mass destruction">possessed weapons of mass destruction</a> and developed <a href="/wiki/Saddam%E2%80%93al-Qaeda_conspiracy_theory" title="Saddam–al-Qaeda conspiracy theory">ties with al-Qaeda</a>.
</p><p>Blair was <a href="/wiki/2005_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="2005 United Kingdom general election">re-elected in 2005</a>, in part thanks to the UK's strong economic performance, but with a substantially reduced majority, in part thanks to the UK's involvement in the Iraq War. During <a href="/wiki/Third_Blair_ministry" title="Third Blair ministry">his third term</a>, Blair pushed for more systemic public sector reform and brokered <a href="/wiki/St_Andrews_Agreement" title="St Andrews Agreement">a settlement</a> to restore powersharing to Northern Ireland. The Afghanistan and Iraq wars continued, and in 2006, Blair announced he would resign within a year. He resigned the party leadership on 24 June 2007 and as prime minister on 27 June, and was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Gordon_Brown" title="Gordon Brown">Gordon Brown</a>, his <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">chancellor</a>. After leaving office, Blair gave up his seat and was appointed Special Envoy of the <a href="/wiki/Quartet_on_the_Middle_East" title="Quartet on the Middle East">Quartet on the Middle East</a>, a diplomatic post he held until 2015. He has been the executive chairman of the <a href="/wiki/Tony_Blair_Institute_for_Global_Change" title="Tony Blair Institute for Global Change">Tony Blair Institute for Global Change</a> since 2016 and has made occasional political interventions. He was knighted by <a href="/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen Elizabeth II">Queen Elizabeth II</a> in 2022.
</p><p>At various points in <a href="/wiki/Premiership_of_Tony_Blair" title="Premiership of Tony Blair">his premiership</a>, Blair was among both the most popular and most unpopular politicians in British history. As prime minister, he achieved the highest recorded approval ratings during his first few years in office but also one of the lowest ratings during and after the Iraq War. Although his critics accuse him of deception and eroding trust in British politics,<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Blair oversaw notable electoral successes and reforms, and is usually rated as above average in <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of the United Kingdom">historical rankings of British prime ministers</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years">Early years</span></h2>
<p>Blair was born at Queen Mary Maternity Home in <a href="/wiki/Edinburgh" title="Edinburgh">Edinburgh</a>, Scotland,<sup id="cite_ref-Edinburgh_Evening-birthplace_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Edinburgh_Evening-birthplace-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> on 6 May 1953.<sup id="cite_ref-whoswho_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-whoswho-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-EB_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He was the second son of <a href="/wiki/Leo_Blair" title="Leo Blair">Leo</a> and Hazel (<i><span title="Name at birth"><a href="/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names" title="Birth name">née</a></span></i> Corscadden) Blair.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Leo Blair was the illegitimate son of two entertainers and was adopted as a baby by the Glasgow shipyard worker James Blair and his wife, Mary.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Hazel Corscadden was the daughter of George Corscadden, a butcher and <a href="/wiki/Orange_Order" title="Orange Order">Orangeman</a> who moved to Glasgow in 1916. In 1923, he returned to (and later died in) <a href="/wiki/Ballyshannon" title="Ballyshannon">Ballyshannon</a>, County Donegal, in <a href="/wiki/Ulster" title="Ulster">Ulster</a>. In Ballyshannon, Corscadden's wife, Sarah Margaret (née Lipsett), gave birth above the family's grocery shop to Blair's mother, Hazel.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Blair has an older brother, <a href="/wiki/William_Blair_(judge)" title="William Blair (judge)">William</a>, and a younger sister, Sarah. Blair's first home was with his family at Paisley Terrace in the Willowbrae area of Edinburgh. During this period, his father worked as a junior tax inspector whilst studying for a law degree from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh" title="University of Edinburgh">University of Edinburgh</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Edinburgh_Evening-birthplace_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Edinburgh_Evening-birthplace-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Blair's first relocation was when he was nineteen months old. At the end of 1954, Blair's parents and their two sons moved from Paisley Terrace to <a href="/wiki/Adelaide" title="Adelaide">Adelaide</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Australia" title="South Australia">South Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-leoguarobit_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leoguarobit-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> His father lectured in law at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Adelaide" title="University of Adelaide">University of Adelaide</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> In Australia, Blair's sister, Sarah, was born. The Blairs lived in the suburb of <a href="/wiki/Dulwich,_South_Australia" title="Dulwich, South Australia">Dulwich</a> close to the university. The family returned to the United Kingdom in mid-1958. They lived for a time with Hazel's mother and stepfather (William McClay) at their home in <a href="/wiki/Stepps" title="Stepps">Stepps</a> on the outskirts of north-east Glasgow. Blair's father accepted a job as a lecturer at <a href="/wiki/Durham_University" title="Durham University">Durham University</a>, and moved the family to <a href="/wiki/Durham,_England" title="Durham, England">Durham, England</a>, when Blair was five. It was the beginning of a long association Blair was to have with Durham.<sup id="cite_ref-leoguarobit_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leoguarobit-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since childhood, Blair has been a fan of <a href="/wiki/Newcastle_United_F.C." title="Newcastle United F.C.">Newcastle United</a> football club.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Education_and_legal_career">Education and legal career</span></h2>
<p>With his parents basing their family in Durham, Blair attended the <a href="/wiki/Chorister_School" title="Chorister School">Chorister School</a> from 1961 to 1966.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Aged 13, he was sent to spend his school term-time boarding at <a href="/wiki/Fettes_College" title="Fettes College">Fettes College</a> in Edinburgh from 1966 to 1971.<sup id="cite_ref-scotfettes_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-scotfettes-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> According to Blair, he hated his time at Fettes.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> His teachers were unimpressed with him; his biographer, <a href="/wiki/John_Rentoul" title="John Rentoul">John Rentoul</a>, reported that "[a]ll the teachers I spoke to when researching the book said he was a complete pain in the backside and they were very glad to see the back of him."<sup id="cite_ref-scotfettes_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-scotfettes-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Blair reportedly modelled himself on <a href="/wiki/Mick_Jagger" title="Mick Jagger">Mick Jagger</a>, lead singer of <a href="/wiki/The_Rolling_Stones" title="The Rolling Stones">The Rolling Stones</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Leaving Fettes College at the age of 18, Blair next spent a gap year in London attempting to find fame as a rock music promoter.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1972, at the age of 19, Blair matriculated at <a href="/wiki/St_John%27s_College,_Oxford" title="St John&#39;s College, Oxford">St John's College, Oxford</a>, reading <a href="/wiki/Jurisprudence" title="Jurisprudence">jurisprudence</a> for three years.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> As a student, he played guitar and sang in a rock band called <a href="/wiki/Ugly_Rumours_(band)" title="Ugly Rumours (band)">Ugly Rumours</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> and performed <a href="/wiki/Stand-up_comedy" title="Stand-up comedy">stand-up comedy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> He was influenced by fellow student and <a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglican</a> priest <a href="/wiki/Peter_Thomson_(priest)" title="Peter Thomson (priest)">Peter Thomson</a>, who awakened his religious faith and left-wing politics. While at Oxford, Blair has stated that he was briefly a <a href="/wiki/Trotskyist" class="mw-redirect" title="Trotskyist">Trotskyist</a>, after reading the first volume of <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Deutscher" title="Isaac Deutscher">Isaac Deutscher</a>'s biography of <a href="/wiki/Leon_Trotsky" title="Leon Trotsky">Leon Trotsky</a>, which was "like a light going on".<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> He graduated from Oxford at the age of 22 in 1975 with a <a href="/wiki/British_undergraduate_degree_classification" title="British undergraduate degree classification">second-class Honours</a> B.A. in jurisprudence.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1975, while Blair was at Oxford, his mother Hazel died aged 52 of thyroid cancer, which greatly affected him.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After Oxford, Blair served his barrister pupillage at <a href="/wiki/Lincoln%27s_Inn" title="Lincoln&#39;s Inn">Lincoln's Inn</a>, where he was called to the Bar. He met his future wife, <a href="/wiki/Cherie_Booth" class="mw-redirect" title="Cherie Booth">Cherie Booth</a>, at the chambers founded by <a href="/wiki/Derry_Irvine,_Baron_Irvine_of_Lairg" title="Derry Irvine, Baron Irvine of Lairg">Derry Irvine</a> (who was to be Blair's first lord chancellor), 11 King's Bench Walk Chambers.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span></h2>
<p>Blair joined the Labour Party shortly after graduating from Oxford in 1975. In the early 1980s, he was involved in Labour politics in <a href="/wiki/Hackney_South_and_Shoreditch_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Hackney South and Shoreditch (UK Parliament constituency)">Hackney South and Shoreditch</a>, where he aligned himself with the "<a href="/wiki/Soft_left" title="Soft left">soft left</a>" of the party. He stood as a candidate for the <a href="/wiki/London_Borough_of_Hackney" title="London Borough of Hackney">Hackney council</a> elections of 1982 in Queensbridge ward, a safe Labour area, but was not selected.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1982, Blair was selected as the Labour Party candidate for the safe Conservative seat of <a href="/wiki/Beaconsfield_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Beaconsfield (UK Parliament constituency)">Beaconsfield</a>, where there was a forthcoming by-election.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Although Blair lost the <a href="/wiki/1982_Beaconsfield_by-election" title="1982 Beaconsfield by-election">Beaconsfield by-election</a> and Labour's share of the vote fell by 10 percentage points, he acquired a profile within the party.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Despite his defeat, William Russell, political correspondent for <i><a href="/wiki/The_Glasgow_Herald" class="mw-redirect" title="The Glasgow Herald">The Glasgow Herald</a></i>, described Blair as "a very good candidate", while acknowledging that the result was "a disaster" for the Labour Party.<sup id="cite_ref-GlasgowHerald28051982_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GlasgowHerald28051982-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> In contrast to his later <a href="/wiki/Centrism" title="Centrism">centrism</a>, Blair made it clear in a letter he wrote to Labour leader <a href="/wiki/Michael_Foot" title="Michael Foot">Michael Foot</a> in July 1982 (published in  2006) that he had "come to Socialism through Marxism" and considered himself on the left.<sup id="cite_ref-LetterTelegraph_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LetterTelegraph-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Like <a href="/wiki/Tony_Benn" title="Tony Benn">Tony Benn</a>, Blair believed that the "Labour right" was bankrupt,<sup id="cite_ref-:0_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> saying "[s]ocialism ultimately must appeal to the better minds of the people. You cannot do that if you are tainted overmuch with a pragmatic period in power."<sup id="cite_ref-LetterTelegraph_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LetterTelegraph-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:0_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Yet, he saw the <a href="/wiki/Hard_left" title="Hard left">hard left</a> as no better, saying:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>There is an arrogance and self-righteousness about many of the groups on the far left which is deeply unattractive to the ordinary would-be member&#160;... There's too much mixing only with people [with] whom they agree.<sup id="cite_ref-LetterTelegraph_36-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LetterTelegraph-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>With a general election due, Blair had not been selected as a candidate anywhere. He was invited to stand again in Beaconsfield, and was initially inclined to agree but was advised by his head of chambers Derry Irvine to find somewhere else which might be winnable.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The situation was complicated by the fact that Labour was fighting a legal action against planned boundary changes, and had selected candidates on the basis of previous boundaries. When the legal challenge failed, the party had to rerun all selections on the new boundaries; most were based on existing seats, but unusually in County Durham a new <a href="/wiki/Sedgefield_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Sedgefield (UK Parliament constituency)">Sedgefield</a> constituency had been created out of Labour-voting areas which had no obvious predecessor seat.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The selection for Sedgefield did not begin until after the <a href="/wiki/1983_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1983 United Kingdom general election">1983 general election</a> was called. Blair's initial inquiries discovered that the left was trying to arrange the selection for <a href="/wiki/Les_Huckfield" title="Les Huckfield">Les Huckfield</a>, sitting MP for Nuneaton who was trying elsewhere; several sitting MPs displaced by boundary changes were also interested in it. When he discovered the <a href="/wiki/Trimdon_Labour_Club" title="Trimdon Labour Club">Trimdon branch</a> had not yet made a nomination, Blair visited them and won the support of the branch secretary <a href="/wiki/John_Burton_(political_agent)" title="John Burton (political agent)">John Burton</a>, and with Burton's help was nominated by the branch. At the last minute, he was added to the shortlist and won the selection over Huckfield. It was the last candidate selection made by Labour before the election, and was made after the Labour Party had issued biographies of all its candidates ("Labour's Election Who's Who").<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John Burton became Blair's <a href="/wiki/Election_agent" title="Election agent">election agent</a> and one of his most trusted and longest-standing allies.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Blair's election literature in the 1983 general election endorsed left-wing policies that Labour advocated in the early 1980s.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> He called for Britain to leave the <a href="/wiki/European_Economic_Community" title="European Economic Community">EEC</a><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> as early as the 1970s,<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> though he had told his selection conference that he personally favoured continuing membership<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and voted "Yes" in the <a href="/wiki/1975_United_Kingdom_European_Communities_membership_referendum" title="1975 United Kingdom European Communities membership referendum">1975 referendum on the subject</a>. He opposed the <a href="/wiki/Exchange_Rate_Mechanism" class="mw-redirect" title="Exchange Rate Mechanism">Exchange Rate Mechanism</a> (ERM) in 1986 but supported the ERM by 1989.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> He was a member of the <a href="/wiki/Campaign_for_Nuclear_Disarmament" title="Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament">Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament</a>, despite never strongly being in favour of <a href="/wiki/Unilateral_nuclear_disarmament" class="mw-redirect" title="Unilateral nuclear disarmament">unilateral nuclear disarmament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Seldon2008_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Seldon2008-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Blair was helped on the campaign trail by soap opera actress <a href="/wiki/Pat_Phoenix" title="Pat Phoenix">Pat Phoenix</a>, his father-in-law's girlfriend. At the age of thirty, he was elected as MP for Sedgefield in 1983; despite the party's <a href="/wiki/1983_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1983 United Kingdom general election">landslide defeat at the general election</a>.
</p><p>In his <a href="/wiki/Maiden_speech" title="Maiden speech">maiden speech</a> in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> on 6 July 1983, Blair stated, "I am a socialist not through reading a textbook that has caught my intellectual fancy, nor through unthinking tradition, but because I believe that, at its best, socialism corresponds most closely to an existence that is both rational and moral. It stands for cooperation, not confrontation; for fellowship, not fear. It stands for equality."<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Once elected, Blair's political ascent was rapid. He received his first <a href="/wiki/Front-bench" class="mw-redirect" title="Front-bench">front-bench</a> appointment in 1984 as assistant <a href="/wiki/HM_Treasury" title="HM Treasury">Treasury</a> spokesman. In May 1985, he appeared on BBC's <i><a href="/wiki/Question_Time_(TV_programme)" title="Question Time (TV programme)">Question Time</a></i>, arguing that the Conservative Government's <a href="/wiki/Public_Order_Act_1986" title="Public Order Act 1986">Public Order White Paper</a> was a threat to civil liberties.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Blair demanded an inquiry into the <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_England" title="Bank of England">Bank of England</a>'s decision to rescue the collapsed <a href="/wiki/Johnson_Matthey" title="Johnson Matthey">Johnson Matthey</a> bank in October 1985. By this time, Blair was aligned with the reforming tendencies in the party (headed by leader <a href="/wiki/Neil_Kinnock" title="Neil Kinnock">Neil Kinnock</a>) and in 1988 was promoted to the shadow <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Trade_and_Industry_(United_Kingdom)" title="Department of Trade and Industry (United Kingdom)">Trade and Industry</a> team as spokesman on the <a href="/wiki/City_of_London" title="City of London">City of London</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leadership_roles">Leadership roles</span></h3>
<p>In 1987, he stood for <a href="/wiki/1987_Labour_Party_Shadow_Cabinet_election" title="1987 Labour Party Shadow Cabinet election">election to the Shadow Cabinet</a>, receiving 71 votes.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> When Kinnock resigned after a fourth consecutive Conservative victory in the <a href="/wiki/1992_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1992 United Kingdom general election">1992 general election</a>, Blair became <a href="/wiki/Shadow_home_secretary" class="mw-redirect" title="Shadow home secretary">shadow home secretary</a> under <a href="/wiki/John_Smith_(Labour_Party_leader)" title="John Smith (Labour Party leader)">John Smith</a>. The old guard argued that trends showed they were regaining strength under Smith's strong leadership.  Meanwhile, the breakaway <a href="/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party" title="Social Democratic Party">SDP</a> faction had merged with the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal Party</a>; the resulting <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_(UK)" title="Liberal Democrats (UK)">Liberal Democrats</a> seemed to pose a major threat to the Labour base. Blair, the leader of the modernising faction, had an entirely different vision, arguing that the long-term trends had to be reversed. The Labour Party was too locked into a base that was shrinking, since it was based on the working-class, on trade unions, and on residents of subsidised council housing. The rapidly growing middle-class was largely ignored, especially the more ambitious working-class families. They aspired to middle-class status but accepted the Conservative argument that Labour was holding ambitious people back with its levelling-down policies. They increasingly saw Labour in terms defined by the opposition, regarding higher taxes and higher interest rates. The steps towards what would become New Labour were procedural but essential. Calling on the slogan "<a href="/wiki/One_member,_one_vote" title="One member, one vote">One member, one vote</a>", John Smith (with limited input from Blair) secured an end to the trade union block vote for Westminster candidate selection at the 1993 conference.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> But Blair and the modernisers wanted Smith to go further still, and called for radical adjustment of Party goals by repealing "<a href="/wiki/Clause_IV" title="Clause IV">Clause IV</a>", the historic commitment to <a href="/wiki/Nationalisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Nationalisation">nationalisation</a> of industry. This would be achieved in 1995.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition">Leader of the Opposition</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Shadow_Cabinet_of_Tony_Blair" title="Shadow Cabinet of Tony Blair">Shadow Cabinet of Tony Blair</a></div>
<p>John Smith died suddenly in 1994 of a heart attack. Blair defeated <a href="/wiki/John_Prescott" title="John Prescott">John Prescott</a> and <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Beckett" title="Margaret Beckett">Margaret Beckett</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1994_Labour_Party_leadership_election" title="1994 Labour Party leadership election">subsequent leadership election</a> and became <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(United_Kingdom)" title="Leader of the Opposition (United Kingdom)">Leader of the Opposition</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Timeline_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Timeline-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> As is customary for the holder of that office, Blair was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">Privy Councillor</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rayment_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rayment-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Felipe_Gonz%C3%A1lez_recibe_al_l%C3%ADder_del_partido_laborista_brit%C3%A1nico._Pool_Moncloa._9_de_abril_de_1996.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Felipe_Gonz%C3%A1lez_recibe_al_l%C3%ADder_del_partido_laborista_brit%C3%A1nico._Pool_Moncloa._9_de_abril_de_1996.jpeg/220px-Felipe_Gonz%C3%A1lez_recibe_al_l%C3%ADder_del_partido_laborista_brit%C3%A1nico._Pool_Moncloa._9_de_abril_de_1996.jpeg" decoding="async" width="220" height="159" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Felipe_Gonz%C3%A1lez_recibe_al_l%C3%ADder_del_partido_laborista_brit%C3%A1nico._Pool_Moncloa._9_de_abril_de_1996.jpeg/330px-Felipe_Gonz%C3%A1lez_recibe_al_l%C3%ADder_del_partido_laborista_brit%C3%A1nico._Pool_Moncloa._9_de_abril_de_1996.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Felipe_Gonz%C3%A1lez_recibe_al_l%C3%ADder_del_partido_laborista_brit%C3%A1nico._Pool_Moncloa._9_de_abril_de_1996.jpeg/440px-Felipe_Gonz%C3%A1lez_recibe_al_l%C3%ADder_del_partido_laborista_brit%C3%A1nico._Pool_Moncloa._9_de_abril_de_1996.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="1452" data-file-height="1052" /></a><figcaption>Blair meeting with Spanish prime minister <a href="/wiki/Felipe_Gonz%C3%A1lez" title="Felipe González">Felipe González</a> at <a href="/wiki/Moncloa_Palace" class="mw-redirect" title="Moncloa Palace">Moncloa Palace</a> in 1996</figcaption></figure>
<p>During his speech at the 1994 Labour Party conference, Blair announced a forthcoming proposal to update the party's objects and objectives, which was widely interpreted to relate to replacing <a href="/wiki/Clause_IV" title="Clause IV">Clause IV</a> of the party's constitution with a new statement of aims and values.<sup id="cite_ref-Timeline_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Timeline-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> This involved the deletion of the party's stated commitment to "the common ownership of the means of production and exchange", which was generally understood to mean wholesale nationalisation of major industries.<sup id="cite_ref-Timeline_53-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Timeline-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-&#39;70s_326_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-&#39;70s_326-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> At a special conference in April 1995, the clause was replaced by a statement that the party is "<a href="/wiki/Democratic_socialist" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic socialist">democratic socialist</a>",<sup id="cite_ref-&#39;70s_326_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-&#39;70s_326-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BarberisMcHugh2000_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BarberisMcHugh2000-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> and Blair also claimed to be a "democratic socialist" himself in the same year.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> However, the move away from nationalisation in the old Clause IV made many on the left wing of the Labour Party feel that Labour was moving away from traditional socialist principles of nationalisation set out in 1918, and was seen by them as part of a shift of the party towards "<a href="/wiki/New_Labour" title="New Labour">New Labour</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He inherited the Labour leadership at a time when the party was ascendant over the Conservatives in the opinion polls, since the Conservative government's reputation in monetary  policy was left in tatters by the <a href="/wiki/Black_Wednesday" title="Black Wednesday">Black Wednesday</a> economic disaster of September 1992. Blair's election as leader saw Labour support surge higher still<sup id="cite_ref-news_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-news-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> in spite of the continuing economic recovery and fall in unemployment that the Conservative government (led by <a href="/wiki/John_Major" title="John Major">John Major</a>) had overseen since the end of the 1990–92 <a href="/wiki/Early_1990s_recession" title="Early 1990s recession">recession</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-news_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-news-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> At the 1996 Labour Party conference, Blair stated that his three top priorities on coming to office were "education, education, and education".<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Aided by the unpopularity of John Major's Conservative government (itself deeply divided over the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> "New Labour" won a landslide victory at the <a href="/wiki/1997_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1997 United Kingdom general election">1997 general election</a>, ending eighteen years of Conservative Party rule, with the heaviest Conservative defeat since <a href="/wiki/1906_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1906 United Kingdom general election">1906</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to diaries released by <a href="/wiki/Paddy_Ashdown" title="Paddy Ashdown">Paddy Ashdown</a>, during Smith's leadership of the Labour Party, there were discussions with Ashdown about forming a <a href="/wiki/Coalition" title="Coalition">coalition</a> government if the next general election resulted in a <a href="/wiki/Hung_parliament" title="Hung parliament">hung parliament</a>. Ashdown also claimed that Blair was a supporter of <a href="/wiki/Proportional_representation" title="Proportional representation">proportional representation</a> (PR).<sup id="cite_ref-BBCPR_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBCPR-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> In addition to Ashdown, Liberal Democrat MPs <a href="/wiki/Menzies_Campbell" title="Menzies Campbell">Menzies Campbell</a> and <a href="/wiki/Alan_Beith" title="Alan Beith">Alan Beith</a> were earmarked for places in the cabinet if a Labour-Lib Dem coalition was formed.<sup id="cite_ref-GuardianPR_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GuardianPR-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Blair was forced to back down on these proposals because John Prescott and <a href="/wiki/Gordon_Brown" title="Gordon Brown">Gordon Brown</a> opposed the PR system, and many members of the Shadow Cabinet were worried about concessions being made towards the Lib Dems.<sup id="cite_ref-GuardianPR_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GuardianPR-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> In the event, virtually every opinion poll since late-1992 put Labour ahead with enough support to form an overall majority.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.281997.E2.80.932007.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(1997–2007)">Prime Minister (1997–2007)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Premiership_of_Tony_Blair" title="Premiership of Tony Blair">Premiership of Tony Blair</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/First_Blair_ministry" title="First Blair ministry">First Blair ministry</a>, <a href="/wiki/Second_Blair_ministry" title="Second Blair ministry">Second Blair ministry</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Third_Blair_ministry" title="Third Blair ministry">Third Blair ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Clinton_Blair.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Clinton_Blair.jpg/220px-Clinton_Blair.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="204" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/cb/Clinton_Blair.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="300" data-file-height="278" /></a><figcaption>Blair with US president <a href="/wiki/Bill_Clinton" title="Bill Clinton">Bill Clinton</a> in Italy, 1999</figcaption></figure>
<p>Blair became the <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of the United Kingdom">prime minister of the United Kingdom</a> on 2 May 1997. Aged 43, Blair became the youngest person to become prime minister since <a href="/wiki/Lord_Liverpool" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Liverpool">Lord Liverpool</a> became prime minister aged 42 in 1812.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> He was also the first prime minister born after <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a> and the accession of <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth&#160;II</a> to the throne. With victories in 1997, 2001, and 2005, Blair was the Labour Party's longest-serving prime minister,<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> and the first and only person to date to lead the party to three consecutive general election victories.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Northern_Ireland">Northern Ireland</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TonyBlairArmagh1998.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/TonyBlairArmagh1998.jpg/220px-TonyBlairArmagh1998.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="175" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c0/TonyBlairArmagh1998.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="300" data-file-height="239" /></a><figcaption>Blair addressing a crowd in <a href="/wiki/Armagh" title="Armagh">Armagh</a>, 1998</figcaption></figure>
<p>His contribution towards assisting the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_peace_process" title="Northern Ireland peace process">Northern Ireland peace process</a> by helping to negotiate the <a href="/wiki/Good_Friday_Agreement" title="Good Friday Agreement">Good Friday Agreement</a> (after 30 years of conflict) was widely recognised.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Following the <a href="/wiki/Omagh_bombing" title="Omagh bombing">Omagh bombing</a> on 15 August 1998, by members of the <a href="/wiki/Real_IRA" class="mw-redirect" title="Real IRA">Real IRA</a> opposed to the peace process, which killed 29 people and wounded hundreds, Blair visited the <a href="/wiki/County_Tyrone" title="County Tyrone">County Tyrone</a> town and met with victims at <a href="/wiki/Royal_Victoria_Hospital,_Belfast" title="Royal Victoria Hospital, Belfast">Royal Victoria Hospital, Belfast</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_intervention_and_the_War_on_Terror">Military intervention and the War on Terror</span></h3>
<p>In his first six years in office, Blair ordered British troops into combat five times, more than any other prime minister in British history. This included Iraq in both <a href="/wiki/1998_bombing_of_Iraq" title="1998 bombing of Iraq">1998</a> and <a href="/wiki/Iraq_War" title="Iraq War">2003</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kosovo_War" title="Kosovo War">Kosovo</a> (1999), <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone_Civil_War" title="Sierra Leone Civil War">Sierra Leone</a> (2000) and <a href="/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)" title="War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)">Afghanistan</a> (2001).<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BushBlairIraqSov.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/BushBlairIraqSov.jpg/220px-BushBlairIraqSov.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="158" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/BushBlairIraqSov.jpg/330px-BushBlairIraqSov.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/BushBlairIraqSov.jpg/440px-BushBlairIraqSov.jpg 2x" data-file-width="514" data-file-height="370" /></a><figcaption>Blair and US president <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a> shake hands at a NATO summit in <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a>, June 2004</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Kosovo War, which Blair had advocated on moral grounds, was initially a failure when it relied solely on air strikes; the threat of a ground offensive convinced Serbia's <a href="/wiki/Slobodan_Milo%C5%A1evi%C4%87" title="Slobodan Milošević">Slobodan Milošević</a> to withdraw. Blair had been a major advocate for a ground offensive, which <a href="/wiki/Bill_Clinton" title="Bill Clinton">Bill Clinton</a> was reluctant to do, and ordered that 50,000 soldiers – most of the available British Army – should be made ready for action.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The following year, the limited <a href="/wiki/Operation_Palliser" class="mw-redirect" title="Operation Palliser">Operation Palliser</a> in Sierra Leone swiftly swung the tide against the rebel forces; before deployment, the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Mission_in_Sierra_Leone" title="United Nations Mission in Sierra Leone">United Nations Mission in Sierra Leone</a> had been on the verge of collapse.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Palliser had been intended as an evacuation mission but <a href="/wiki/David_Richards,_Baron_Richards_of_Herstmonceux" title="David Richards, Baron Richards of Herstmonceux">Brigadier David Richards</a> was able to convince Blair to allow him to expand the role; at the time, Richards' action was not known and Blair was assumed to be behind it.<sup id="cite_ref-Little_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Little-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Blair ordered <a href="/wiki/Operation_Barras" title="Operation Barras">Operation Barras</a>, a highly successful <a href="/wiki/Special_Air_Service" title="Special Air Service">SAS</a>/<a href="/wiki/Parachute_Regiment_(United_Kingdom)" title="Parachute Regiment (United Kingdom)">Parachute Regiment</a> strike to rescue hostages from a Sierra Leone rebel group.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Journalist <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Marr" title="Andrew Marr">Andrew Marr</a> has argued that the success of ground attacks, real and threatened, over air strikes alone was influential on how Blair planned the Iraq War, and that the success of the first three wars Blair fought "played to his sense of himself as a moral war leader".<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> When asked in 2010 if the success of Palliser may have "embolden[ed] British politicians" to think of military action as a policy option, General Sir David Richards admitted there "might be something in that".<sup id="cite_ref-Little_78-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Little-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Blair_Bush_Whitehouse_(2004-11-12).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Blair_Bush_Whitehouse_%282004-11-12%29.jpg/220px-Blair_Bush_Whitehouse_%282004-11-12%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="142" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Blair_Bush_Whitehouse_%282004-11-12%29.jpg/330px-Blair_Bush_Whitehouse_%282004-11-12%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Blair_Bush_Whitehouse_%282004-11-12%29.jpg/440px-Blair_Bush_Whitehouse_%282004-11-12%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="515" data-file-height="332" /></a><figcaption>Blair and US president <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a> shake hands after their press conference in the <a href="/wiki/East_Room" title="East Room">East Room</a> of the White House, November 2004</figcaption></figure>
<p>From the start of the <a href="/wiki/War_on_Terror" class="mw-redirect" title="War on Terror">War on Terror</a> in 2001, Blair strongly supported the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_United_States" title="Foreign policy of the United States">foreign policy</a> of <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a>, participating in the <a href="/wiki/2001_invasion_of_Afghanistan" class="mw-redirect" title="2001 invasion of Afghanistan">2001 invasion of Afghanistan</a> and <a href="/wiki/2003_invasion_of_Iraq" title="2003 invasion of Iraq">2003 invasion of Iraq</a>. The invasion of Iraq was particularly controversial, as it attracted widespread public opposition and 139 of Blair's own MPs opposed it.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a result, he faced criticism over the policy itself and the circumstances of the decision. <a href="/wiki/Alastair_Campbell" title="Alastair Campbell">Alastair Campbell</a> described Blair's statement that the intelligence on WMDs was "beyond doubt" as his "assessment of the assessment that was given to him."<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> In 2009, Blair stated that he would have supported removing <a href="/wiki/Saddam_Hussein" title="Saddam Hussein">Saddam Hussein</a> from power even in the face of proof that he had no such weapons.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Playwright <a href="/wiki/Harold_Pinter" title="Harold Pinter">Harold Pinter</a> and former Malaysian Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Mahathir_Mohamad" title="Mahathir Mohamad">Mahathir Mohamad</a> accused Blair of war crimes.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Testifying before the <a href="/wiki/Iraq_Inquiry" title="Iraq Inquiry">Iraq Inquiry</a> on 29 January 2010, Blair said Saddam was a "monster and I believe he threatened not just the region but the world."<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Blair said that British and American attitude towards Saddam Hussein had "changed dramatically" after the <a href="/wiki/September_11_attacks" title="September 11 attacks">11 September attacks</a>. Blair denied that he would have supported the invasion of Iraq even if he had thought Saddam had no weapons of mass destruction. He said he believed the world was safer as a result of the invasion.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> He said there was "no real difference between wanting regime change and wanting Iraq to disarm: regime change was US policy because Iraq was in breach of its UN obligations."<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> In an October 2015 <a href="/wiki/CNN" title="CNN">CNN</a> interview with <a href="/wiki/Fareed_Zakaria" title="Fareed Zakaria">Fareed Zakaria</a>, Blair apologised for his "mistakes" over Iraq War and admitted there were "elements of truth" to the view that the invasion helped promote the rise of <a href="/wiki/ISIS" class="mw-redirect" title="ISIS">ISIS</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Chilcot_Inquiry" class="mw-redirect" title="Chilcot Inquiry">Chilcot Inquiry</a> report of 2016 gave a damning assessment of Blair's role in the Iraq War, though the former prime minister again refused to apologise for his decision to back the US-led invasion.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relationship_with_Parliament">Relationship with Parliament</span></h3>
<p>One of Blair's first acts as prime minister was to replace the then twice-weekly 15-minute sessions of <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister%27s_Questions" title="Prime Minister&#39;s Questions">Prime Minister's Questions</a> held on Tuesdays and Thursdays with a single 30-minute session on Wednesdays. In addition to PMQs, Blair held monthly press conferences at which he fielded questions from journalists<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> and – from 2002 – broke precedent by agreeing to give evidence twice yearly before the most senior Commons select committee, the <a href="/wiki/Liaison_Committee_(House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom)" title="Liaison Committee (House of Commons of the United Kingdom)">Liaison Committee</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Blair was sometimes perceived as paying insufficient attention both to the views of his own Cabinet colleagues and to those of the House of Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> His style was sometimes criticised as not that of a prime minister and <a href="/wiki/Head_of_government" title="Head of government">head of government</a>, which he was, but of a president and head of state – which he was not.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Blair was accused of excessive reliance on <a href="/wiki/Spin_(political)" class="mw-redirect" title="Spin (political)">spin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Wheatcroft_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wheatcroft-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> He was the first UK prime minister to have been formally questioned by police, though not <a href="/wiki/Right_to_silence_in_England_and_Wales" title="Right to silence in England and Wales">under caution</a>, while still in office.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Events_before_resignation">Events before resignation</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For a chronological guide, see <a href="/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2007_Labour_Party_leadership_election_(UK)" title="Timeline of the 2007 Labour Party leadership election (UK)">Timeline of the 2007 Labour Party leadership election (UK)</a>.</div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Blair_visiting_Poland_April_07.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Blair_visiting_Poland_April_07.jpg/220px-Blair_visiting_Poland_April_07.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="217" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Blair_visiting_Poland_April_07.jpg/330px-Blair_visiting_Poland_April_07.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Blair_visiting_Poland_April_07.jpg/440px-Blair_visiting_Poland_April_07.jpg 2x" data-file-width="560" data-file-height="552" /></a><figcaption>Blair in Poland, 2007</figcaption></figure>
<p>As the <a href="/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Iraq_War" title="Casualties of the Iraq War">casualties of the Iraq War</a> mounted, Blair was accused of misleading Parliament,<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> and his popularity dropped as a result,<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> with Labour's overall majority at the <a href="/wiki/2005_UK_election" class="mw-redirect" title="2005 UK election">2005 election</a> reduced from 167 to 66 seats. As a combined result of the <a href="/wiki/Blair%E2%80%93Brown_pact" class="mw-redirect" title="Blair–Brown pact">Blair–Brown pact</a>, the Iraq War and low approval ratings, pressure built up within the Labour Party for Blair to resign.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Over the summer of 2006, many MPs criticised Blair for not calling for a ceasefire in the <a href="/wiki/2006_Israel%E2%80%93Lebanon_conflict" class="mw-redirect" title="2006 Israel–Lebanon conflict">Israel–Lebanon conflict</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> On 7 September 2006, Blair publicly stated he would step down as leader by the time of the <a href="/wiki/Trades_Union_Congress" title="Trades Union Congress">Trades Union Congress</a> conference held from 10 to 13 September 2007,<sup id="cite_ref-iwillquit_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-iwillquit-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> despite promising to serve a full term during the previous general election campaign. On 10 May 2007, during a speech at the <a href="/wiki/Trimdon_Labour_Club" title="Trimdon Labour Club">Trimdon Labour Club</a>, Blair announced his intention to resign as both Labour leader and prime minister,<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> triggering <a href="/wiki/2007_Labour_Party_leadership_election_(UK)" title="2007 Labour Party leadership election (UK)">a leadership election</a> in which Brown was the only candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At a special party conference in Manchester on 24 June 2007, Blair formally handed over the leadership of the Labour Party to Brown, who had been Chancellor of the Exchequer in Blair's three ministries.<sup id="cite_ref-Brown_is_UK&#39;s_new_prime_minister_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brown_is_UK&#39;s_new_prime_minister-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Blair tendered his resignation as prime minister on 27 June and Brown assumed office the same afternoon. Blair resigned from his Sedgefield seat in the traditional form of accepting the <a href="/wiki/Stewardship_of_the_Chiltern_Hundreds" class="mw-redirect" title="Stewardship of the Chiltern Hundreds">Stewardship of the Chiltern Hundreds</a>, to which he was appointed by Brown in one of the latter's last acts as chancellor;<sup id="cite_ref-Independent27June2007_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Independent27June2007-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> the resulting <a href="/wiki/2007_Sedgefield_by-election" title="2007 Sedgefield by-election">by-election</a> was won by Labour candidate <a href="/wiki/Phil_Wilson_(British_politician)" title="Phil Wilson (British politician)">Phil Wilson</a>. Blair decided not to issue a list of <a href="/wiki/Resignation_Honours" class="mw-redirect" title="Resignation Honours">Resignation Honours</a>, making him the first prime minister of the modern era not to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Policies">Policies</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Blairism" title="Blairism">Blairism</a></div>
<p>In 2001, Blair said, "We are a <a href="/wiki/Centre-left_politics" title="Centre-left politics">left of centre</a> party, pursuing economic prosperity and social justice as partners and not as opposites".<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Blair rarely applies such labels to himself, but he promised before the 1997 election that New Labour would govern "from the radical centre", and according to one lifelong Labour Party member, has always described himself as a <a href="/wiki/Social_democracy" title="Social democracy">social democrat</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> However, in a 2007 opinion piece in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Guardian" title="The Guardian">The Guardian</a></i>, left-wing commentator Neil Lawson described Blair as to the <a href="/wiki/Centre-right_politics" title="Centre-right politics">right of centre</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> A <a href="/wiki/YouGov" title="YouGov">YouGov</a> opinion poll in 2005 found that a small majority of British voters, including many New Labour supporters, placed Blair on the right of the political spectrum.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> The <i><a href="/wiki/Financial_Times" title="Financial Times">Financial Times</a></i> on the other hand has argued that Blair is not conservative, but instead a <a href="/wiki/Populist" class="mw-redirect" title="Populist">populist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Critics and admirers tend to agree that Blair's electoral success was based on his ability to occupy the centre ground and appeal to voters across the political spectrum, to the extent that he has been fundamentally at odds with traditional Labour Party values. Some left-wing critics, such as <a href="/wiki/Mike_Marqusee" title="Mike Marqusee">Mike Marqusee</a> in 2001, argued that Blair oversaw the final stage of a long term shift of the Labour Party to the right.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There is some evidence that Blair's long term dominance of the centre forced his Conservative opponents to shift a long distance to the left to challenge his <a href="/wiki/Hegemony" title="Hegemony">hegemony</a> there.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Leading Conservatives of the post-New Labour era hold Blair in high regard: <a href="/wiki/George_Osborne" title="George Osborne">George Osborne</a> describes him as "the master", <a href="/wiki/Michael_Gove" title="Michael Gove">Michael Gove</a> thought he had an "entitlement to conservative respect" in February 2003, while <a href="/wiki/David_Cameron" title="David Cameron">David Cameron</a> reportedly maintained Blair as an informal adviser.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_reforms">Social reforms</span></h3>
<p>Blair introduced significant constitutional reforms; promoted new rights for gay people; and signed treaties integrating Britain more closely with the EU. With specifical regards to Blair's <a href="/wiki/LGBTQ%2B" class="mw-redirect" title="LGBTQ+">LGBTQ+</a> reforms, Blair introduced the <a href="/wiki/Civil_Partnership_Act_2004" title="Civil Partnership Act 2004">Civil Partnership Act 2004</a> which granted civil partners rights and responsibilities similar to those in civil marriages, equalised the age of consent between straight and gay couples, ended the ban on gay people serving in the British military, introduced the <a href="/wiki/Gender_Recognition_Act_2004" title="Gender Recognition Act 2004">Gender Recognition Act 2004</a> which allows those with <a href="/wiki/Gender_dysphoria" title="Gender dysphoria">gender dysphoria</a> to legally change their gender, repealed <a href="/wiki/Section_28" title="Section 28">Section 28</a>, gave gay couples the right to adopt and enacted several anti-discrimination policies into law.
</p><p>The New Labour government increased police powers by adding to the number of arrestable offences, compulsory <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_National_DNA_Database" title="United Kingdom National DNA Database">DNA recording</a> and the use of dispersal orders.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> Under Blair's government the amount of new legislation increased<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> which attracted criticism.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> He also introduced tough anti-terrorism and <a href="/wiki/Identity_Cards_Act_2006" title="Identity Cards Act 2006">identity card legislation</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_policies">Economic policies</span></h3>
<p>Tony Blair has been credited with overseeing a strong economy, with real incomes of British citizens growing 18% between 1997 and 2006. Britain saw rapid productivity growth and significant GDP growth, as well as falling poverty rates and inequality which, despite stubbornly failing to fall, stalled thanks to New Labour's economic policies (such as tax credits). Despite the financial bubble developing in the property markets, studies have credited the growth to investments in education and the maintenance of fiscal responsibility, rather than a financial sugar-high.<sup id="cite_ref-LSE_2011-11-15_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LSE_2011-11-15-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:World_Economic_Forum_Annual_Meeting_2005a.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/World_Economic_Forum_Annual_Meeting_2005a.jpg/220px-World_Economic_Forum_Annual_Meeting_2005a.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="133" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/World_Economic_Forum_Annual_Meeting_2005a.jpg/330px-World_Economic_Forum_Annual_Meeting_2005a.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/06/World_Economic_Forum_Annual_Meeting_2005a.jpg/440px-World_Economic_Forum_Annual_Meeting_2005a.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="1856" /></a><figcaption>Blair at the <a href="/wiki/World_Economic_Forum" title="World Economic Forum">World Economic Forum</a> in Davos, 2005</figcaption></figure>
<p>During his time as prime minister, Blair kept direct taxes low, while raising indirect taxation; invested a significant amount in <a href="/wiki/Human_capital" title="Human capital">Human capital</a>; introduced a <a href="/wiki/National_Minimum_Wage" class="mw-redirect" title="National Minimum Wage">National Minimum Wage</a> and some new employment rights (while keeping <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a>'s trade union reforms);<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>. He introduced substantial market-based reforms in the education and health sectors; introduced student <a href="/wiki/Tuition_fees_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Tuition fees in the United Kingdom">tuition fees</a>; introduced a welfare to work scheme and sought to reduce certain categories of welfare payments. He did not reverse the <a href="/wiki/Impact_of_the_privatisation_of_British_Rail" title="Impact of the privatisation of British Rail">privatisation of the railways</a> enacted by his predecessor John Major and instead strengthened regulation (by creating the <a href="/wiki/Office_of_Rail_Regulation" class="mw-redirect" title="Office of Rail Regulation">Office of Rail Regulation</a>) and limited fare rises to <a href="/wiki/Retail_price_index" class="mw-redirect" title="Retail price index">inflation</a> +1%.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NHS_Spending_1948-2014.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/NHS_Spending_1948-2014.png/250px-NHS_Spending_1948-2014.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="181" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/NHS_Spending_1948-2014.png/375px-NHS_Spending_1948-2014.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/NHS_Spending_1948-2014.png/500px-NHS_Spending_1948-2014.png 2x" data-file-width="865" data-file-height="626" /></a><figcaption>NHS spending 1948/49 to 2014/15<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Blair and Brown raised spending on the NHS and other public services, increasing spending from 39.9% of GDP to 48.1% in 2010–11.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> They pledged in 2001 to bring NHS spending to the levels of other European countries, and doubled spending in real terms to over £100 billion in England alone.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Immigration">Immigration</span></h3>
<p>Non-European immigration rose significantly during the period from 1997, not least because of the <a href="/wiki/First_Blair_ministry" title="First Blair ministry">government</a>'s abolition of the primary purpose rule in June 1997.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> This change made it easier for UK residents to bring foreign spouses into the country.
The former government advisor Andrew Neather in the <i><a href="/wiki/Evening_Standard" title="Evening Standard">Evening Standard</a></i> stated that the deliberate policy of ministers from late 2000 until early 2008 was to open up the UK to mass migration.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Neather later stated that his words had been twisted, saying: "The main goal was to allow in more migrant workers at a point when – hard as it is to imagine now – the booming economy was running up against skills shortages.... Somehow this has become distorted by excitable Right-wing newspaper columnists into being a "plot" to make Britain multicultural. There was no plot."<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Environmental_record">Environmental record</span></h3>
<p>Blair criticised other governments for not doing enough to solve <a href="/wiki/Global_climate_change" class="mw-redirect" title="Global climate change">global climate change</a>. In a 1997 visit to the United States, he made a comment on "great industrialised nations" that fail to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. Again in 2003, Blair went before the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Congress" title="United States Congress">United States Congress</a> and said that climate change "cannot be ignored", insisting "we need to go beyond even <a href="/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol" title="Kyoto Protocol">Kyoto</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Blair and his party promised a 20% reduction in carbon dioxide.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> The Labour Party also claimed that by 2010 10% of the energy would come from renewable resources; however, it only reached 7% by that point.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2000, Blair "flagged up" 100&#160;million euros for green policies and urged environmentalists and businesses to work together.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Chirac_Bush_Blair_Berlusconi.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Chirac_Bush_Blair_Berlusconi.jpg/220px-Chirac_Bush_Blair_Berlusconi.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="145" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Chirac_Bush_Blair_Berlusconi.jpg/330px-Chirac_Bush_Blair_Berlusconi.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Chirac_Bush_Blair_Berlusconi.jpg/440px-Chirac_Bush_Blair_Berlusconi.jpg 2x" data-file-width="750" data-file-height="495" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Jacques_Chirac" title="Jacques Chirac">Jacques Chirac</a>, <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a>, Blair and <a href="/wiki/Silvio_Berlusconi" title="Silvio Berlusconi">Silvio Berlusconi</a> during the <a href="/wiki/29th_G8_summit" title="29th G8 summit">G8 Summit</a> in <a href="/wiki/%C3%89vian-les-Bains" title="Évian-les-Bains">Évian</a>, 2003</figcaption></figure>
<p>Blair built his foreign policy on basic principles (close ties with U.S. and E.U.) and added a new activist philosophy of "interventionism". In 2001 Britain joined the U.S. in the global war on terror.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Blair forged friendships with several European leaders, including <a href="/wiki/Silvio_Berlusconi" title="Silvio Berlusconi">Silvio Berlusconi</a> of Italy,<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Angela_Merkel" title="Angela Merkel">Angela Merkel</a> of Germany<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> and later <a href="/wiki/Nicolas_Sarkozy" title="Nicolas Sarkozy">Nicolas Sarkozy</a> of France.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2005_03_01_rice_blair_600.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/2005_03_01_rice_blair_600.jpg/220px-2005_03_01_rice_blair_600.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="215" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/2005_03_01_rice_blair_600.jpg/330px-2005_03_01_rice_blair_600.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/2005_03_01_rice_blair_600.jpg/440px-2005_03_01_rice_blair_600.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="587" /></a><figcaption>Blair meets with US secretary of state <a href="/wiki/Condoleezza_Rice" title="Condoleezza Rice">Condoleezza Rice</a> in 2005</figcaption></figure>
<p>Along with enjoying a close relationship with Bill Clinton, Blair formed a strong political alliance with George W. Bush, particularly in the area of foreign policy. For his part, Bush lauded Blair and the UK.
